The input document

1. The water cycle describes how water moves between the atmosphere, the land, and bodies of water. Oceans, lakes, rivers, soil, groundwater, snow, and ice store water in reservoirs. Evaporation changes liquid water into water vapor, while condensation changes vapor back into liquid. Solar energy supplies much of the energy needed for evaporation from oceans, lakes, and moist ground. The rate also depends on humidity, wind, and the availability of water. Plants absorb water through their roots and release some of it as vapor through leaf openings called stomata. This release is transpiration. Evapotranspiration combines evaporation from surfaces with transpiration by plants. Moving air transports water vapor. When air cools sufficiently and becomes saturated, vapor can condense onto small particles to form droplets. Clouds contain tiny liquid droplets, ice crystals, or both; they are not simply invisible water vapor. Cloud particles must grow before they can fall as precipitation, and some falling water evaporates before reaching the ground. Rain and snow return atmospheric water to the surface.

2. After precipitation reaches land, water can follow several pathways. Some remains on leaves and later evaporates, some flows over the surface, and some enters the ground. Infiltration is the entry of water through the ground surface into soil or other porous material. Soil structure, existing moisture, vegetation, and rainfall intensity affect how quickly infiltration occurs. Water in soil may be absorbed by roots, remain in pore spaces, or move deeper through the subsurface. This deeper movement is called percolation. Water reaching the saturated zone can contribute to groundwater recharge, but not all infiltrating water reaches that zone. Groundwater fills pores or fractures in saturated material. An aquifer is a geologic formation that stores and transmits usable quantities of groundwater. Groundwater can move through connected openings and discharge into streams, lakes, or springs. A spring occurs where groundwater emerges at the surface. These connections allow groundwater to maintain flow in some streams during dry periods, linking underground storage to visible surface water.

3. Surface runoff develops when water flows over the land, including when rainfall exceeds the ground's ability to absorb it or when the ground is already saturated. Gravity moves runoff downslope toward channels, streams, and rivers. A watershed is the land area that drains surface water toward a common outlet. Rivers carry water downstream, while lakes and wetlands can temporarily store it and influence the timing of its movement. Snow and ice also store water; melting releases liquid water that can enter streams or soil. Storage and movement occur over different timescales, so water does not follow one compulsory loop. A water budget compares inputs, outputs, and changes in storage within a defined area and time interval. Human activities can alter this balance. Paved surfaces often reduce infiltration and increase rapid runoff. Pumping removes groundwater, and sustained withdrawals greater than replenishment can reduce groundwater storage. Understanding these pathways helps explain how rainfall, underground reserves, and downstream water availability are connected.

Rejected · vapor condensation → acts_on → droplet

Origin: document

Proposal: Quoted extraction proposed for semantic review

Quote: to form droplets

Reviewer: The relation claims 'vapor condensation' acts on 'droplet'. The quote states vapor condenses *to form* droplets. This implies the creation of the droplet, not an action upon an existing droplet. The target 'droplet' is the result, not the object being acted upon in a pre-existing state. The relation definition requires TARGET to be an object whose state/position is changed; here the droplet is created by the process, making the 'acts_on' relation semantically invalid for this specific causal direction (creation vs modification).

The claim must be rejected due to improper predicate application.

chunk_001 · reconstruction score (0–100) 49.066

Original

The water cycle describes how water moves between the atmosphere, the land, and bodies of water. Oceans, lakes, rivers, soil, groundwater, snow, and ice store water in reservoirs. Evaporation changes liquid water into water vapor, while condensation changes vapor back into liquid. Solar energy supplies much of the energy needed for evaporation from oceans, lakes, and moist ground. The rate also depends on humidity, wind, and the availability of water.

Reconstruction

The water cycle describes how water moves between the atmosphere, the land, and bodies of water. Solar energy supplies energy for evaporation from oceans, lakes, and moist ground, causing liquid water to change into water vapor. Condensation causes vapor to change into liquid. Oceans, lakes, rivers, soil, groundwater, snow, and ice all store water.

The passage was constructed by first establishing the primary definition of the 'water cycle' using its relation to the movement concept. Next, the energy input mechanism ('solar energy') and its specific effect ('evaporation') were linked, including the condition of origin and modality provided in the data. The causal link between evaporation and the phase change was added. Finally, all entities identified as 'storing' water (ocean, lake, river, soil, groundwater, snow, ice) were listed to complete the description of the system's components.

chunk_002 · reconstruction score (0–100) not evaluated

Original

Plants absorb water through their roots and release some of it as vapor through leaf openings called stomata. This release is transpiration. Evapotranspiration combines evaporation from surfaces with transpiration by plants. Moving air transports water vapor. When air cools sufficiently and becomes saturated, vapor can condense onto small particles to form droplets. Clouds contain tiny liquid droplets, ice crystals, or both; they are not simply invisible water vapor.

Reconstruction

A plant emits vapor through leaf openings called stomata. Air cooling sufficiently enables air saturation, which can enable vapor condensation. A cloud contains tiny liquid droplets.

chunk_004 · reconstruction score (0–100) 86.698

Original

After precipitation reaches land, water can follow several pathways. Some remains on leaves and later evaporates, some flows over the surface, and some enters the ground. Infiltration is the entry of water through the ground surface into soil or other porous material. Soil structure, existing moisture, vegetation, and rainfall intensity affect how quickly infiltration occurs. Water in soil may be absorbed by roots, remain in pore spaces, or move deeper through the subsurface. This deeper movement is called percolation.

Reconstruction

Precipitation reaches land. Water remains on a leaf within the leaf structure. Water flows over a surface within the surface area. Water enters the ground within the ground layer. Infiltration is defined as the entry of water through the ground surface into soil or other porous material. The rate of infiltration is influenced by soil structure, existing moisture, vegetation, and rainfall intensity. Deeper movement describes percolation.

The text was constructed by sequentially stating the arrival of precipitation at land, followed by the occurrence of specific water behaviors (remaining on leaves, flowing over surfaces, entering ground) within their respective locations. The definition of infiltration was included to clarify the process. Finally, all factors influencing the rate of infiltration were listed as per the provided relations, concluding with the description of deeper movement as percolation.

chunk_005 · reconstruction score (0–100) 33.450

Original

Water reaching the saturated zone can contribute to groundwater recharge, but not all infiltrating water reaches that zone. Groundwater fills pores or fractures in saturated material. An aquifer is a geologic formation that stores and transmits usable quantities of groundwater. Groundwater can move through connected openings and discharge into streams, lakes, or springs. A spring occurs where groundwater emerges at the surface. These connections allow groundwater to maintain flow in some streams during dry periods, linking underground storage to visible surface water.

Reconstruction

Infiltrating water reaches the saturated zone, though not all of it does so. Water reaching the saturated zone contributes to groundwater recharge. An aquifer stores usable quantities of groundwater. Underground storage links to visible surface water via connections that allow groundwater to maintain flow in some streams during dry periods. A spring occurs where groundwater emerges at the surface.

The passage was constructed by sequentially linking concepts based on the provided relations: 'infiltrating water' reaches the 'saturated zone' (with the condition 'not all'), which then contributes to 'groundwater recharge'. The 'aquifer' stores 'groundwater' (condition: 'usable quantities'). 'Underground storage' links to 'visible surface water' (condition: 'via connections that allow groundwater to maintain flow', modality: 'allow'), specifically enabling flow in some streams during dry periods. Finally, a 'spring' occurs in the location of 'groundwater emergence at the surface'. All conditions and modalities from the data were preserved without inference.

chunk_006 · reconstruction score (0–100) 54.938

Original

Surface runoff develops when water flows over the land, including when rainfall exceeds the ground's ability to absorb it or when the ground is already saturated. Gravity moves runoff downslope toward channels, streams, and rivers. A watershed is the land area that drains surface water toward a common outlet. Rivers carry water downstream, while lakes and wetlands can temporarily store it and influence the timing of its movement. Snow and ice also store water; melting releases liquid water that can enter streams or soil.

Reconstruction

Surface runoff develops when water flow over the land occurs, specifically including conditions where rainfall exceeds the ground's ability to absorb it or when the ground is already saturated. Rainfall exceeds the absorption capacity of the ground, leading to surface runoff. A river transports water downstream. A lake can temporarily store water and can influence the rate of timing of movement. A wetland can temporarily store water and can influence the rate of timing of movement. Snow and ice stores water. Melting releases liquid water into the environment. Liquid water can enter a stream or can enter soil.

chunk_007 · reconstruction score (0–100) 62.801

Original

Storage and movement occur over different timescales, so water does not follow one compulsory loop. A water budget compares inputs, outputs, and changes in storage within a defined area and time interval. Human activities can alter this balance. Paved surfaces often reduce infiltration and increase rapid runoff. Pumping removes groundwater, and sustained withdrawals greater than replenishment can reduce groundwater storage. Understanding these pathways helps explain how rainfall, underground reserves, and downstream water availability are connected.

Reconstruction

A water budget describes input, output, and change in storage within a defined area and time interval. Water does not follow one compulsory loop. Human activity can act on balance. Paved surface often inhibits infiltration.

The passage synthesizes the 'describes' relations by listing what a water budget accounts for (input, output, change in storage) under their shared condition ('within a defined area and time interval'). The 'does_not_follow' relation is stated as a fact about water. The 'acts_on' relation regarding human activity includes its modality ('can'). The 'inhibits' relation regarding paved surfaces includes its condition ('often'). All predicates are derived strictly from the provided relation definitions.
